T16 · CLAUDE'S REPLY:
I ran all six checks and changed nothing. The analysis itself holds up: Table 1, Table 2 and the methods all match the code and outputs. The problems are in the text, mostly the paragraphs carried over from `notes/draft.md`:
- **Deb & Trivedi reference:** the page range should be 601–625, not 601–652.
- **Aron-Dine et al.:** the memo says they found no effect. They concluded the opposite.
- **"A single city, Seattle":** wrong; the experiment ran at six sites.
- **"The average is 3.9 outpatient visits":** the data give 2.86.
- **Two "at least" figures** are rounded up, so they overstate.
- **The 100% coinsurance rows** in the data conflict with the memo saying only rates up to 95% were tested.

**1. References against Crossref**

| Field | Memo | Crossref | Match? |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Cameron & Trivedi (10.1017/CBO9780511811241)** | | | |
| Authors | Cameron, A. C., & Trivedi, P. K. | A. Colin Cameron; Pravin K. Trivedi | ✓ |
| Year | 2005 | 2005 (print); 2012 online | ✓ |
| Title | *Microeconometrics: Methods and applications* | "Microeconometrics" / "Methods and Applications" | ✓ |
| Publisher | Cambridge University Press | Cambridge University Press | ✓ |
| DOI | 10.1017/CBO9780511811241 | same, lowercased | ✓ |
| **Deb & Trivedi (10.1016/S0167-6296(02)00008-5)** | | | |
| Authors | Deb, P., & Trivedi, P. K. | Partha Deb; Pravin K. Trivedi | ✓ |
| Year | 2002 | July 2002 | ✓ |
| Title | The structure of demand for health care: Latent class versus two-part models | same | ✓ |
| Journal, volume, issue | *J. Health Economics*, 21(4) | same | ✓ |
| **Pages** | **601–652** | **601–625** | ✗ |
| DOI | as given | same | ✓ |

I also checked Aron-Dine et al. while I was at it: authors, title, journal, 27(1), pages 197–222, 2013 and DOI all match.

**2. Do citing sentences describe their sources accurately?**
- **Aron-Dine, Einav & Finkelstein (2013): ✗ inaccurate.**
  - The memo says they "conclude that cost sharing had no effect on how often people saw a doctor."
  - They conclude the opposite: "the RAND data still reject the null hypothesis of no utilization response to cost sharing," including for outpatient spending.
  - They also don't report a doctor-visit estimate specifically.
- **Deb & Trivedi (2002): ✓ broadly accurate.** "Use the same data to model the demand for doctor visits."
  - The paper models demand for health care with RAND HIE data; a later paper describes it that way, and Cameron & Trivedi's data are based on it.
  - I couldn't reach the full text or abstract (ScienceDirect blocked the request), so this rests on a secondary description.
- **Cameron & Trivedi (2005): ~ mostly accurate.** The data are "as distributed by" them.
  - The statsmodels documentation says the data were obtained from Cameron & Trivedi's site.
  - It also says they are "only a subset of the original data" and "vary slightly compared to that reported in Cameron and Trivedi."

**3. Factual claims about the experiment and the data**

| Claim | Verdict | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Families were assigned to plans at random | ✓ | Aron-Dine et al.; RAND |
| "The experiment ran in a single city, Seattle" (Data) | ✗ Six sites: Dayton; Seattle; Fitchburg–Leominster and Franklin County, Mass.; Charleston and Georgetown County, S.C. | ICPSR 6439; Aron-Dine et al. |
| One row per person-year with visits, coinsurance, deductible and health measures | ✓ | statsmodels documentation |
| Incentive payments differed by plan | ✓ Larger lump sums for less generous plans | Aron-Dine et al. |
| No one enrolled more than 5 years (Methods); plans ran 3 or 5 years (Limitations) | ✓ Sources say 3–5 years | Aron-Dine et al. |
| "The experiment tested coinsurance rates of 0%, 25%, 50%, and 95%" (Interpretation) | ~ Incomplete; see below | Aron-Dine et al. |

On the coinsurance rates:
- **Other plans:** the experiment also had a mixed 25%/50% plan, and an individual-deductible plan with 95% outpatient and 0% inpatient coinsurance. Assignment to the 50% plan was stopped partway through.
- **The 100% rows:** the data code 1,074 person-years at 100% coinsurance, all in deductible plans. That conflicts with the memo saying the results say nothing about rates "above 95%," since 100% observations went into the estimate.

**4. Numbers in the text against the data and code**

| Memo says | Recomputed | Verdict |
|---|---|---|
| 20,190 person-years | 20,190 | ✓ |
| Average 3.9 visits (Data) | **2.86** | ✗ |
| About 8% fewer (IRR 0.916); about 26% fewer (IRR 0.739) | 8.4%; 26.1% | ✓ |
| Free care to 95%: about a third fewer, 3.3 to 2.2 visits | 0.671; 3.33 to 2.23 | ✓ |
| Rate ratios 0.75 / 0.71 / 0.67; worst-case intervals [0.69, 0.82], [0.63, 0.79], [0.59, 0.76] | 0.752 / 0.709 / 0.671; intervals match | ✓ |
| "At least 18%" fewer in the 25% plan | **17.5%** | ✗ Rounded up, which overstates a lower bound; should say at least 17% |
| "At least 24%" fewer in the 95% plan | **23.7%** | ✗ Same problem; should say at least 23% |
| Deductible 0.74; 3.1 vs 2.3 visits | 0.739; 3.09 vs 2.28 | ✓ |
| Visits vary about six times what Poisson assumes | χ²/df = 6.3 | ✓ |

| Exploratory: 9% fewer; 1.1 fewer vs 0.4 more; 302 (1.5%); 207 free; fewer than 100 with coinsurance | 0.913; −1.11 / +0.38; 302 (1.50%); 207; 95 | ✓ |
| Table 2 p-values: < .001, .70, .86, .004, .20 | 1e-44 / 4e-10, .697, .862, .0039, .196 | ✓ |

Person-years are stated the same way throughout: "20,190 person-years" in the Summary and the exploratory section, and "Person-years 20,190" in Table 1. The only other count, 16,844 in the influential-observations column, is labeled as that column's count.

**5. Results description against Table 1: ✓**
- **Table 1:** every cell in the memo's Table 1 matches `output/table1.md`; only the variable names are made readable.
- **The text:** every statement agrees with the table. Both cost-sharing effects are significant under the worst-case standard errors in all three columns. The negative binomial estimates are 0.917 and 0.749, and the check without influential observations gives 0.885 and 0.618. Physical limitation and chronic diseases go with more visits.
- **Table 2:** matches `output/exploratory_poor_health.md`.

**6. Methods against `analysis.py`, and plan account against `plan.md`: ✓, with one tension**
- **Methods:** the memo matches the code: Poisson, the stated variables plus `lpi`, all person-years, rate ratios, robust (HC0) standard errors, and the √5 bound. The robustness checks match too: negative binomial, and Cook's distance above 4/n refit as Poisson.
- **Pre-analysis plan section:** matches the original five items in `plan.md`.
- **Departures section:** matches `plan.md`'s "Departures from the plan": the standard-error changes, `lpi` with your reason, and the two robustness checks with the exception for the dropping check. Both say these were decided after the first results.
- **The exploratory analysis:** labeled as not in the plan in both files.
- **The tension:** your Limitations paragraph says the standard errors "treat each person-year as independent, so they are too small." That's true of the robust standard errors. But the memo's main inference uses the worst-case √5 bound, which is designed to cover exactly this. You may want to acknowledge that bound in the paragraph, so it doesn't read as an unaddressed flaw.

**Not checkable:** the 5-year enrollment cap can't be confirmed from the data, because there's no person ID. It rests on the sources above.

      output: {"filePath": "/private/var/folders/_k/wpbxz8gn451dmsv_93z4yt6r0000gn/T/wd-scripted2-rand-low-high-spz7tur5/project/memo.md", "newString": "Aron-Dine, Einav, and Finkelstein (2013) revisit the experiment and conclude\nthat its data still reject the hypothesis that use of care does not respond\nto cost sharing, including for outpatient spending, while cautioning against\nsummarizing the effects with a single price elasticity.", "oldString": "Aron-Dine, Einav, and Finkelstein (2013) revisit the experiment and conclude\nthat cost sharing had no effect on how often people saw a doctor.", "originalFile": "# Does cost sharing reduce doctor visits? Evidence from the RAND Health Insurance Experiment\n\n## Summary\n\nWe test whether people in insurance plans with higher coinsurance make fewer\noutpatient doctor visits, using 20,190 person-years from the RAND Health\nInsurance Experiment. They do. In a Poisson regression, each unit of log\ncoinsurance goes with about 8% fewer visits (incidence-rate ratio [IRR] 0.916),\nand an individual deductible with about 26% fewer (IRR 0.739). Moving from\nfree care to 95% coinsurance goes with about a third fewer visits, from 3.3 to\n2.2 a year on average. Both effects are significant at the .05 level\n(two-tailed) even under a worst-case allowance for the same people appearing\nin several years, and they hold up in two robustness checks. An exploratory\nanalysis suggests that people in poor health may not cut back, but that\nevidence is weak.\n\n## Background\n\nWhen people pay part of the cost of their care, do they use less of it? The\nRAND Health Insurance Experiment answered this by assigning families at\nrandom to insurance plans that differed in how much of the bill they paid.\nAron-Dine, Einav, and Finkelstein (2013) revisit the experiment and conclude\nthat cost sharing had no effect on how often people saw a doctor. Deb and\nTrivedi (2002) use the same data to model the demand for doctor visits.\n\n## Data\n\nThe experiment ran in a single city, Seattle. The data, as distributed by\nCameron and Trivedi (2005), have one row per person-year, with the number of\noutpatient doctor visits, the plan's coinsurance rate, whether the plan had\nan individual deductible, and measures of health. Across person-years, the\naverage is 3.9 outpatient visits.\n\n## Pre-analysis plan\n\nBefore looking at the outcome data, we fixed the analysis in a written plan.\nThe hypothesis was that people in plans with higher coinsurance make fewer\noutpatient doctor visits. The outcome is the number of outpatient visits\n(`mdvis`). The treatment is the plan's log coinsurance (`lncoins`) and whether\nit has an individual deductible (`idp`). The controls are self-rated health\n(good, fair, or poor, against excellent), physical limitation, and the number\nof chronic diseases. The model is a Poisson regression.\n\n## Methods\n\nWe estimate the number of outpatient visits with a Poisson regression on the\nplan's log coinsurance rate and whether it had an individual deductible,\ncontrolling for self-rated health, physical limitation, the number of\nchronic diseases, and the participation incentive, which differed by plan. We report incidence-rate ratios and keep every person-year,\nincluding those with no visits. Because visits are overdispersed, we use\nheteroskedasticity-robust standard errors.\n\nMost people appear in the data for several years, so their observations are\nnot independent. The data have no person identifier, so we cannot cluster\nstandard errors by person, and with these data there is no better option. We\ninstead report a worst case: since no one was enrolled for more than five\nyears, standard errors clustered by person could be at most √5 times the\nrobust ones, and we show results with the robust standard errors inflated by\nthat factor.\n\n## Departures from the plan\n\nAll of the following were decided after the first results had been seen.\n\n- **Standard errors.** The plan did not say which standard errors to use.\n Visits vary about six times as much as a Poisson model assumes, so we\n replaced model-based standard errors with robust ones, and added the √5\n worst-case bound for repeated people.\n- **Participation incentive.** We added the participation incentive as a\n control. Incentive payments differed by plan and could themselves affect\n visits, so leaving them out could mix their effect into the estimated effect\n of cost sharing.\n- **Robustness checks.** The plan named none. We added two (see below). One\n of them drops person-years, a deliberate exception, for that check only, to\n our rule of keeping every person-year.\n\n## Results\n\nTable 1 reports the main model in its first column. All rows are\nincidence-rate ratios: values below 1 mean fewer visits.\n\n**Table 1. Outpatient visits: main model and robustness checks**\n\n| Variable | Main: Poisson | Robustness: without influential obs. | Robustness: negative binomial |\n|---|---:|---:|---:|\n| Log coinsurance | 0.916* | 0.885* | 0.917* |\n| | (0.006) [0.013] | (0.004) [0.009] | (0.006) [0.013] |\n| Individual deductible | 0.739* | 0.618* | 0.749* |\n| | (0.020) [0.045] | (0.012) [0.026] | (0.020) [0.045] |\n| Health good | 0.988 | 0.926* | 0.961 |\n| | (0.022) [0.050] | (0.015) [0.033] | (0.022) [0.050] |\n| Health fair | 1.056 | 1.043 | 1.013 |\n| | (0.045) [0.100] | (0.026) [0.058] | (0.043) [0.096] |\n| Health poor | 1.238 | 1.421* | 1.213 |\n| | (0.095) [0.213] | (0.050) [0.112] | (0.088) [0.197] |\n| Physical limitation | 1.299* | 1.455* | 1.300* |\n| | (0.043) [0.096] | (0.027) [0.061] | (0.043) [0.097] |\n| Chronic diseases | 1.035* | 1.047* | 1.040* |\n| | (0.002) [0.004] | (0.001) [0.002] | (0.002) [0.004] |\n| Participation incentive | 1.024* | 1.038* | 1.026* |\n| | (0.005) [0.010] | (0.003) [0.007] | (0.005) [0.010] |\n| Constant (baseline rate) | 1.986* | 1.232* | 1.891* |\n| | (0.057) [0.127] | (0.025) [0.056] | (0.054) [0.121] |\n| Dispersion (alpha) | | | 1.305 (0.023) |\n| Person-years | 20,190 | 16,844 | 20,190 |\n\n*Notes:* Incidence-rate ratios. Robust (HC0) standard errors in parentheses;\nworst-case standard errors (robust × √5) in brackets; both transformed by the\ndelta method. * p < .05, two-tailed, using the worst-case standard error. The\ncheck without influential observations drops the 3,346 person-years whose\nCook's distance in the main model exceeds 4/n. Alpha is the negative binomial\ndispersion parameter, not exponentiated.\n\n**Interpretation.** The results support the hypothesis. Holding health and the\nparticipation incentive fixed, people in plans with more cost sharing made\nfewer outpatient visits. The experiment tested coinsurance rates of 0% (free\ncare), 25%, 50%, and 95%, and we interpret the estimate only by comparing\nthese plans with one another. Compared with free care, the model gives the\nfollowing rate ratios for expected visits:\n\n| Plan | Rate ratio vs. free care | 95% CI (worst case) |\n|---|---:|---:|\n| 25% coinsurance | 0.75 | [0.69, 0.82] |\n| 50% coinsurance | 0.71 | [0.63, 0.79] |\n| 95% coinsurance | 0.67 | [0.59, 0.76] |\n\nThat is, about 25%, 29%, and 33% fewer visits than under free care. Averaged\nover the sample, expected visits fall from 3.3 a year under free care to 2.2\nunder 95% coinsurance. Even the upper ends of the worst-case intervals imply\nfewer visits than under free care in every tested plan, by at least 18% in\nthe 25% plan and at least 24% in the 95% plan.\n\nTwo cautions keep this reading within what was tested. First, the model\nenters coinsurance in logs, so the shape of the pattern across plans, with\nmost of the reduction at the first step away from free care, comes from that\nchoice of form; it was not estimated plan by plan. Second, the results say\nnothing about coinsurance rates the experiment did not offer, such as rates\nbetween 50% and 95% or above 95%.\n\nPlans with an individual deductible had about a quarter fewer visits than\nplans without one, holding the coinsurance rate fixed in the model (rate\nratio 0.74; on average,\n3.1 visits a year without a deductible and 2.3 with one).\n\nBecause plans were assigned at random, these differences can be read as\neffects of cost sharing rather than of who chose which plan. The controls\nbehave as expected: people with physical limitations or more chronic\ndiseases see the doctor more often.\n\n## Robustness\n\nThe **negative binomial** model allows visits to vary more than the Poisson\nmodel does, which matters here because visits vary about six times as much\nas a Poisson model assumes. It gives almost the same cost-sharing estimates\n(0.917 for coinsurance, 0.749 for the deductible), so the main result does\nnot depend on the Poisson assumption.\n\nThe model **without influential observations** gives larger effects (0.885\nand 0.618). This check should be read with care: it drops 3,346 person-years\n(17%), mostly heavy users averaging 8.1 visits a year, along with 737\nperson-years with no visits. It describes the effect among lighter users more\nthan it tests the stability of the main estimate. In all three columns the\ncost-sharing effects point the same way and are significant under the\nworst-case standard errors.\n\n## Exploratory analysis: people in poor health\n\n*Exploratory. This analysis was not in the pre-analysis plan and was chosen\nafter the confirmatory results had been seen. It can suggest a pattern but\ncannot confirm one.*\n\nPeople in poor health may have less room to cut back on doctor visits, so\ncost sharing might reduce their visits less. We added an interaction between\nlog coinsurance and poor self-rated health to the main Poisson model, keeping\nevery other choice the same.\n\n**Table 2 (exploratory). Coinsurance effect by poor self-rated health**\n\n| Estimate | IRR | 95% CI (robust) | p | 95% CI (worst case) | p (worst case) |\n|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|\n| Log coinsurance, not in poor health | 0.913 | [0.902, 0.925] | < .001 | [0.888, 0.940] | < .001 |\n| Log coinsurance, in poor health | 1.014 | [0.945, 1.088] | .70 | [0.866, 1.188] | .86 |\n| Ratio of the two | 1.110 | [1.034, 1.192] | .004 | [0.947, 1.301] | .20 |\n\nAmong people not in poor health, each unit of log coinsurance goes with about\n9% fewer visits. Among people in poor health, the estimate is close to no\neffect. The difference between the two is significant with robust standard\nerrors but not under the worst-case bound for repeated people. In predicted\nvisits, moving from free care to 95% coinsurance goes with 1.1 fewer visits a\nyear for people not in poor health, and 0.4 more for people in poor health.\n\nThis evidence is weak. Only 302 of 20,190 person-years (1.5%) are in poor\nhealth, and 207 of those are in the free-care plan, so the poor-health\nestimate rests on fewer than 100 person-years with any coinsurance. Poor\nhealth was not randomized, so people who rate their health as poor may differ\nin other ways that also shape how they respond to cost sharing. The pattern\nis consistent with the idea that sicker people cut back less, but it would\nneed confirmation in data with more people in poor health and with person\nidentifiers.\n\n## Limitations\n\nWithout a person identifier, the standard errors treat each person-year as independent, so they are too small. The plans ran for three or five years, and people may have delayed or moved care around the plan's start and end.\n\n## Conclusion\n\nCost sharing reduced outpatient doctor visits in the RAND Health Insurance\nExperiment. People facing 95% coinsurance made about a third fewer visits\nthan people with free care, and an individual deductible reduced visits by\nabout a quarter. The result holds under a worst-case allowance for repeated\nobservations of the same people and across both robustness checks. Data with\nperson and family identifiers would allow properly clustered standard errors,\nand a larger sample of people in poor health would be needed to test whether\ncost sharing affects them differently.\n\n## References\n\nAron-Dine, A., Einav, L., & Finkelstein, A. (2013). The RAND Health Insurance Experiment, three decades later. *Journal of Economic Perspectives*, 27(1), 197–222. https://doi.org/10.1257/jep.27.1.197\n\nCameron, A. C., & Trivedi, P. K. (2005). *Microeconometrics: Methods and applications*. Cambridge University Press. https://doi.org/10.1017/CBO9780511811241\n\nDeb, P., & Trivedi, P. K. (2002). The structure of demand for health care: Latent class versus two-part models. *Journal of Health Economics*, 21(4), 601–652. https://doi.org/10.1016/S0167-6296(02)00008-5\n", "replaceAll": false, "structuredPatch": [{"lines": [" RAND Health Insurance Experiment answered this by assigning families at", " random to insurance plans that differed in how much of the bill they paid.", " Aron-Dine, Einav, and Finkelstein (2013) revisit the experiment and conclude", "-that cost sharing had no effect on how often people saw a doctor. Poor\nhealth was not randomized, so people who rate their health as poor may differ\nin other ways that also shape how they respond to cost sharing. The pattern\nis consistent with the idea that sicker people cut back less, but it would\nneed confirmation in data with more people in poor health and with person\nidentifiers.\n\n## Limitations\n\nWithout a person identifier, the standard errors treat each person-year as independent, so they are too small. The plans ran for three or five years, and people may have delayed or moved care around the plan's start and end.\n\n## Conclusion\n\nCost sharing reduced outpatient doctor visits in the RAND Health Insurance\nExperiment. People facing 95% coinsurance made about a third fewer visits\nthan people with free care, and an individual deductible reduced visits by\nabout a quarter. The result holds under a worst-case allowance for repeated\nobservations of the same people and across both robustness checks. Data with\nperson and family identifiers would allow properly clustered standard errors,\nand a larger sample of people in poor health would be needed to test whether\ncost sharing affects them differently.\n\n## References\n\nAron-Dine, A., Einav, L., & Finkelstein, A. (2013). The RAND Health Insurance Experiment, three decades later. *Journal of Economic Perspectives*, 27(1), 197–222. https://doi.org/10.1257/jep.27.1.197\n\nCameron, A. C., & Trivedi, P. K. (2005). *Microeconometrics: Methods and applications*. Cambridge University Press. https://doi.org/10.1017/CBO9780511811241\n\nDeb, P., & Trivedi, P. K. (2002).       output: {"filePath": "/private/var/folders/_k/wpbxz8gn451dmsv_93z4yt6r0000gn/T/wd-scripted2-rand-low-high-spz7tur5/project/memo.md", "newString": "fewer outpatient visits. We interpret the estimate\nonly by comparing free care with the 25%, 50%, and 95% plans.", "oldString": "fewer outpatient visits. The experiment tested coinsurance rates of 0% (free\ncare), 25%, 50%, and 95%, and we interpret the estimate only by comparing\nthese plans with one another.", "originalFile": "# Does cost sharing reduce doctor visits? Evidence from the RAND Health Insurance Experiment\n\n## Summary\n\nWe test whether people in insurance plans with higher coinsurance make fewer\noutpatient doctor visits, using 20,190 person-years from the RAND Health\nInsurance Experiment. They do. In a Poisson regression, each unit of log\ncoinsurance goes with about 8% fewer visits (incidence-rate ratio [IRR] 0.916),\nand an individual deductible with about 26% fewer (IRR 0.739). Moving from\nfree care to 95% coinsurance goes with about a third fewer visits, from 3.3 to\n2.2 a year on average. Both effects are significant at the .05 level\n(two-tailed) even under a worst-case allowance for the same people appearing\nin several years, and they hold up in two robustness checks. An exploratory\nanalysis suggests that people in poor health may not cut back, but that\nevidence is weak.\n\n## Background\n\nWhen people pay part of the cost of their care, do they use less of it? The\nRAND Health Insurance Experiment answered this by assigning families at\nrandom to insurance plans that differed in how much of the bill they paid.\nAron-Dine, Einav, and Finkelstein (2013) revisit the experiment and conclude\nthat its data still reject the hypothesis that use of care does not respond\nto cost sharing, including for outpatient spending, while cautioning against\nsummarizing the effects with a single price elasticity. Deb and\nTrivedi (2002) use the same data to model the demand for doctor visits.\n\n## Data\n\nThe experiment ran at six sites: Dayton, Ohio; Seattle, Washington;\nFitchburg–Leominster and Franklin County, Massachusetts; and Charleston and\nGeorgetown County, South Carolina. The data, a subset of those distributed by\nCameron and Trivedi (2005), have one row per person-year, with the number of\noutpatient doctor visits, the plan's coinsurance rate, whether the plan had\nan individual deductible, and measures of health. Across person-years, the\naverage is 2.86 outpatient visits.\n\n## Pre-analysis plan\n\nBefore looking at the outcome data, we fixed the analysis in a written plan.\nThe hypothesis was that people in plans with higher coinsurance make fewer\noutpatient doctor visits. The outcome is the number of outpatient visits\n(`mdvis`). The treatment is the plan's log coinsurance (`lncoins`) and whether\nit has an individual deductible (`idp`). The controls are self-rated health\n(good, fair, or poor, against excellent), physical limitation, and the number\nof chronic diseases. The model is a Poisson regression.\n\n## Methods\n\nWe estimate the number of outpatient visits with a Poisson regression on the\nplan's log coinsurance rate and whether it had an individual deductible,\ncontrolling for self-rated health, physical limitation, the number of\nchronic diseases, and the participation incentive, which differed by plan. We report incidence-rate ratios and keep every person-year,\nincluding those with no visits. Because visits are overdispersed, we use\nheteroskedasticity-robust standard errors.\n\nMost people appear in the data for several years, so their observations are\nnot independent. The data have no person identifier, so we cannot cluster\nstandard errors by person, and with these data there is no better option. We\ninstead report a worst case: since no one was enrolled for more than five\nyears, standard errors clustered by person could be at most √5 times the\nrobust ones, and we show results with the robust standard errors inflated by\nthat factor.\n\n## Departures from the plan\n\nAll of the following were decided after the first results had been seen.\n\n- **Standard errors.** The plan did not say which standard errors to use.\n Visits vary about six times as much as a Poisson model assumes, so we\n replaced model-based standard errors with robust ones, and added the √5\n worst-case bound for repeated people.\n- **Participation incentive.** We added the participation incentive as a\n control. Incentive payments differed by plan and could themselves affect\n visits, so leaving them out could mix their effect into the estimated effect\n of cost sharing.\n- **Robustness checks.** The plan named none. We added two (see below). One\n of them drops person-years, a deliberate exception, for that check only, to\n our rule of keeping every person-year.\n\n## Results\n\nTable 1 reports the main model in its first column. All rows are\nincidence-rate ratios: values below 1 mean fewer visits.\n\n**Table 1. Outpatient visits: main model and robustness checks**\n\n| Variable | Main: Poisson | Robustness: without influential obs. | Robustness: negative binomial |\n|---|---:|---:|---:|\n| Log coinsurance | 0.916* | 0.885* | 0.917* |\n| | (0.006) [0.013] | (0.004) [0.009] | (0.006) [0.013] |\n| Individual deductible | 0.739* | 0.618* | 0.749* |\n| | (0.020) [0.045] | (0.012) [0.026] | (0.020) [0.045] |\n| Health good | 0.988 | 0.926* | 0.961 |\n| | (0.022) [0.050] | (0.015) [0.033] | (0.022) [0.050] |\n| Health fair | 1.056 | 1.043 | 1.013 |\n| | (0.045) [0.100] | (0.026) [0.058] | (0.043) [0.096] |\n| Health poor | 1.238 | 1.421* | 1.213 |\n| | (0.095) [0.213] | (0.050) [0.112] | (0.088) [0.197] |\n| Physical limitation | 1.299* | 1.455* | 1.300* |\n| | (0.043) [0.096] | (0.027) [0.061] | (0.043) [0.097] |\n| Chronic diseases | 1.035* | 1.047* | 1.040* |\n| | (0.002) [0.004] | (0.001) [0.002] | (0.002) [0.004] |\n| Participation incentive | 1.024* | 1.038* | 1.026* |\n| | (0.005) [0.010] | (0.003) [0.007] | (0.005) [0.010] |\n| Constant (baseline rate) | 1.986* | 1.232* | 1.891* |\n| | (0.057) [0.127] | (0.025) [0.056] | (0.054) [0.121] |\n| Dispersion (alpha) | | | 1.305 (0.023) |\n| Person-years | 20,190 | 16,844 | 20,190 |\n\n*Notes:* Incidence-rate ratios. Robust (HC0) standard errors in parentheses;\nworst-case standard errors (robust × √5) in brackets; both transformed by the\ndelta method. * p < .05, two-tailed, using the worst-case standard error. The\ncheck without influential observations drops the 3,346 person-years whose\nCook's distance in the main model exceeds 4/n. Alpha is the negative binomial\ndispersion parameter, not exponentiated.\n\n**Interpretation.** The results support the hypothesis. Holding health and the\nparticipation incentive fixed, people in plans with more cost sharing made\nfewer outpatient visits. The experiment tested coinsurance rates of 0% (free\ncare), 25%, 50%, and 95%, and we interpret the estimate only by comparing\nthese plans with one another. Compared with free care, the model gives the\nfollowing rate ratios for expected visits:\n\n| Plan | Rate ratio vs. free care | 95% CI (worst case) |\n|---|---:|---:|\n| 25% coinsurance | 0.75 | [0.69, 0.82] |\n| 50% coinsurance | 0.71 | [0.63, 0.79] |\n| 95% coinsurance | 0.67 | [0.59, 0.76] |\n\nThat is, about 25%, 29%, and 33% fewer visits than under free care. Averaged\nover the sample, expected visits fall from 3.3 a year under free care to 2.2\nunder 95% coinsurance. Even the upper ends of the worst-case intervals imply\nfewer visits than under free care in every tested plan, by at least 18% in\nthe 25% plan and at least 24% in the 95% plan.\n\nTwo cautions keep this reading within what was tested. First, the model\nenters coinsurance in logs, so the shape of the pattern across plans, with\nmost of the reduction at the first step away from free care, comes from that\nchoice of form; it was not estimated plan by plan. Second, the results say\nnothing about coinsurance rates the experiment did not offer, such as rates\nbetween 50% and 95% or above 95%.\n\nPlans with an individual deductible had about a quarter fewer visits than\nplans without one, holding the coinsurance rate fixed in the model (rate\nratio 0.74; on average,\n3.1 visits a year without a deductible and 2.3 with one).\n\nBecause plans were assigned at random, these differences can be read as\neffects of cost sharing rather than of who chose which plan. The controls\nbehave as expected: people with physical limitations or more chronic\ndiseases see the doctor more often.\n\n## Robustness\n\nThe **negative binomial** model allows visits to vary more than the Poisson\nmodel does, which matters here because visits vary about six times as much\nas a Poisson model assumes. It gives almost the same cost-sharing estimates\n(0.917 for coinsurance, 0.749 for the deductible), so the main result does\nnot depend on the Poisson assumption.\n\nThe model **without influential observations** gives larger effects (0.885\nand 0.618). This check should be read with care: it drops 3,346 person-years\n(17%), mostly heavy users averaging 8.1 visits a year, along with 737\nperson-years with no visits. It describes the effect among lighter users more\nthan it tests the stability of the main estimate. In all three columns the\ncost-sharing effects point the same way and are significant under the\nworst-case standard errors.\n\n## Exploratory analysis: people in poor health\n\n*Exploratory. This analysis was not in the pre-analysis plan and was chosen\nafter the confirmatory results had been seen. It can suggest a pattern but\ncannot confirm one.*\n\nPeople in poor health may have less room to cut back on doctor visits, so\ncost sharing might reduce their visits less. We added an interaction between\nlog coinsurance and poor self-rated health to the main Poisson model, keeping\nevery other choice the same.\n\n**Table 2 (exploratory). Coinsurance effect by poor self-rated health**\n\n| Estimate | IRR | 95% CI (robust) | p | 95% CI (worst case) | p (worst case) |\n|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|\n| Log coinsurance, not in poor health | 0.913 | [0.902, 0.925] | < .001 | [0.888, 0.940] | < .001 |\n| Log coinsurance, in poor health | 1.014 | [0.945, 1.088] | .70 | [0.866, 1.188] | .86 |\n| Ratio of the two | 1.110 | [1.034, 1.192] | .004 | [0.947, 1.301] | .20 |\n\nAmong people not in poor health, each unit of log coinsurance goes with about\n9% fewer visits. Among people in poor health, the estimate is close to no\neffect. The difference between the two is significant with robust standard\nerrors but not under the worst-case bound for repeated people. In predicted\nvisits, moving from free care to 95% coinsurance goes with 1.1 fewer visits a\nyear for people not in poor health, and 0.4 more for people in poor health.\n\nThis evidence is weak. Only 302 of 20,190 person-years (1.5%) are in poor\nhealth, and 207 of those are in the free-care plan, so the poor-health\nestimate rests on fewer than 100 person-years with any coinsurance. Poor\nhealth was not randomized, so people who rate their health as poor may differ\nin other ways that also shape how they respond to cost sharing. The pattern\nis consistent with the idea that sicker people cut back less, but it would\nneed confirmation in data with more people in poor health and with person\nidentifiers.\n\n## Limitations\n\nWithout a person identifier, the standard errors treat each person-year as independent, so they are too small. The plans ran for three or five years, and people may have delayed or moved care around the plan's start and end.\n\n## Conclusion\n\nCost sharing reduced outpatient doctor visits in the RAND Health Insurance\nExperiment. People facing 95% coinsurance made about a third fewer visits\nthan people with free care, and an individual deductible reduced visits by\nabout a quarter. The result holds under a worst-case allowance for repeated\nobservations of the same people and across both robustness checks. Data with\nperson and family identifiers would allow properly clustered standard errors,\nand a larger sample of people in poor health would be needed to test whether\ncost sharing affects them differently.\n\n## References\n\nAron-Dine, A., Einav, L., & Finkelstein, A. (2013). The RAND Health Insurance Experiment, three decades later. *Journal of Economic Perspectives*, 27(1), 197–222. https://doi.org/10.1257/jep.27.1.197\n\nCameron, A. C., & Trivedi, P. K. (2005). *Microeconometrics: Methods and applications*. Cambridge University Press. https://doi.org/10.1017/CBO9780511811241\n\nDeb, P., & Trivedi, P. K. (2002). The structure of demand for health care: Latent class versus two-part models. *Journal of Health Economics*, 21(4), 601–652. https://doi.org/10.1016/S0167-6296(02)00008-5\n", "replaceAll": false, "structuredPatch": [{"lines": [" ", " **Interpretation.** The results support the hypothesis. The plans ran for three or five years, and people may have delayed or moved care around the plan's start and end.\n\n## Conclusion\n\nCost sharing reduced outpatient doctor visits in the RAND Health Insurance\nExperiment. People facing 95% coinsurance made about a third fewer visits\nthan people with free care, and an individual deductible reduced visits by\nabout a quarter. The result holds under a worst-case allowance for repeated\nobservations of the same people and across both robustness checks. Data with\nperson and family identifiers would allow properly clustered standard errors,\nand a larger sample of people in poor health would be needed to test whether\ncost sharing affects them differently.\n\n## References\n\nAron-Dine, A., Einav, L., & Finkelstein, A. Second, the results say\nnothing about coinsurance rates the experiment did not offer, such as rates\nbetween 50% and 95% or above 95%.", "originalFile": "# Does cost sharing reduce doctor visits? Evidence from the RAND Health Insurance Experiment\n\n## Summary\n\nWe test whether people in insurance plans with higher coinsurance make fewer\noutpatient doctor visits, using 20,190 person-years from the RAND Health\nInsurance Experiment. They do. In a Poisson regression, each unit of log\ncoinsurance goes with about 8% fewer visits (incidence-rate ratio [IRR] 0.916),\nand an individual deductible with about 26% fewer (IRR 0.739). Moving from\nfree care to 95% coinsurance goes with about a third fewer visits, from 3.3 to\n2.2 a year on average. Both effects are significant at the .05 level\n(two-tailed) even under a worst-case allowance for the same people appearing\nin several years, and they hold up in two robustness checks. An exploratory\nanalysis suggests that people in poor health may not cut back, but that\nevidence is weak.\n\n## Background\n\nWhen people pay part of the cost of their care, do they use less of it? The\nRAND Health Insurance Experiment answered this by assigning families at\nrandom to insurance plans that differed in how much of the bill they paid.\nAron-Dine, Einav, and Finkelstein (2013) revisit the experiment and conclude\nthat its data still reject the hypothesis that use of care does not respond\nto cost sharing, including for outpatient spending, while cautioning against\nsummarizing the effects with a single price elasticity. Deb and\nTrivedi (2002) use the same data to model the demand for doctor visits.\n\n## Data\n\nThe experiment ran at six sites: Dayton, Ohio; Seattle, Washington;\nFitchburg–Leominster and Franklin County, Massachusetts; and Charleston and\nGeorgetown County, South Carolina. The data, a subset of those distributed by\nCameron and Trivedi (2005), have one row per person-year, with the number of\noutpatient doctor visits, the plan's coinsurance rate, whether the plan had\nan individual deductible, and measures of health. Across person-years, the\naverage is 2.86 outpatient visits.\n\n## Pre-analysis plan\n\nBefore looking at the outcome data, we fixed the analysis in a written plan.\nThe hypothesis was that people in plans with higher coinsurance make fewer\noutpatient doctor visits. The outcome is the number of outpatient visits\n(`mdvis`). The treatment is the plan's log coinsurance (`lncoins`) and whether\nit has an individual deductible (`idp`). The controls are self-rated health\n(good, fair, or poor, against excellent), physical limitation, and the number\nof chronic diseases. The model is a Poisson regression.\n\n## Methods\n\nWe estimate the number of outpatient visits with a Poisson regression on the\nplan's log coinsurance rate and whether it had an individual deductible,\ncontrolling for self-rated health, physical limitation, the number of\nchronic diseases, and the participation incentive, which differed by plan. We report incidence-rate ratios and keep every person-year,\nincluding those with no visits. Because visits are overdispersed, we use\nheteroskedasticity-robust standard errors.\n\nMost people appear in the data for several years, so their observations are\nnot independent. The data have no person identifier, so we cannot cluster\nstandard errors by person, and with these data there is no better option. We\ninstead report a worst case: since no one was enrolled for more than five\nyears, standard errors clustered by person could be at most √5 times the\nrobust ones, and we show results with the robust standard errors inflated by\nthat factor.\n\n## Departures from the plan\n\nAll of the following were decided after the first results had been seen.\n\n- **Standard errors.** The plan did not say which standard errors to use.\n Visits vary about six times as much as a Poisson model assumes, so we\n replaced model-based standard errors with robust ones, and added the √5\n worst-case bound for repeated people.\n- **Participation incentive.** We added the participation incentive as a\n control. Incentive payments differed by plan and could themselves affect\n visits, so leaving them out could mix their effect into the estimated effect\n of cost sharing.\n- **Robustness checks.** The plan named none. We added two (see below). One\n of them drops person-years, a deliberate exception, for that check only, to\n our rule of keeping every person-year.\n\n## Results\n\nTable 1 reports the main model in its first column. All rows are\nincidence-rate ratios: values below 1 mean fewer visits.\n\n**Table 1. Outpatient visits: main model and robustness checks**\n\n| Variable | Main: Poisson | Robustness: without influential obs. | Robustness: negative binomial |\n|---|---:|---:|---:|\n| Log coinsurance | 0.916* | 0.885* | 0.917* |\n| | (0.006) [0.013] | (0.004) [0.009] | (0.006) [0.013] |\n| Individual deductible | 0.739* | 0.618* | 0.749* |\n| | (0.020) [0.045] | (0.012) [0.026] | (0.020) [0.045] |\n| Health good | 0.988 | 0.926* | 0.961 |\n| | (0.022) [0.050] | (0.015) [0.033] | (0.022) [0.050] |\n| Health fair | 1.056 | 1.043 | 1.013 |\n| | (0.045) [0.100] | (0.026) [0.058] | (0.043) [0.096] |\n| Health poor | 1.238 | 1.421* | 1.213 |\n| | (0.095) [0.213] | (0.050) [0.112] | (0.088) [0.197] |\n| Physical limitation | 1.299* | 1.455* | 1.300* |\n| | (0.043) [0.096] | (0.027) [0.061] | (0.043) [0.097] |\n| Chronic diseases | 1.035* | 1.047* | 1.040* |\n| | (0.002) [0.004] | (0.001) [0.002] | (0.002) [0.004] |\n| Participation incentive | 1.024* | 1.038* | 1.026* |\n| | (0.005) [0.010] | (0.003) [0.007] | (0.005) [0.010] |\n| Constant (baseline rate) | 1.986* | 1.232* | 1.891* |\n| | (0.057) [0.127] | (0.025) [0.056] | (0.054) [0.121] |\n| Dispersion (alpha) | | | 1.305 (0.023) |\n| Person-years | 20,190 | 16,844 | 20,190 |\n\n*Notes:* Incidence-rate ratios. Robust (HC0) standard errors in parentheses;\nworst-case standard errors (robust × √5) in brackets; both transformed by the\ndelta method. * p < .05, two-tailed, using the worst-case standard error. The\ncheck without influential observations drops the 3,346 person-years whose\nCook's distance in the main model exceeds 4/n. Alpha is the negative binomial\ndispersion parameter, not exponentiated.\n\n**Interpretation.** The results support the hypothesis. Holding health and the\nparticipation incentive fixed, people in plans with more cost sharing made\nfewer outpatient visits. The plans in these data have coinsurance rates of\n0% (free care), 25%, 50%, and 95%; another 1,074 person-years, all in plans\nwith an individual deductible, are coded at 100%. We interpret the estimate\nonly by comparing free care with the 25%, 50%, and 95% plans. Compared with free care, the model gives the\nfollowing rate ratios for expected visits:\n\n| Plan | Rate ratio vs. free care | 95% CI (worst case) |\n|---|---:|---:|\n| 25% coinsurance | 0.75 | [0.69, 0.82] |\n| 50% coinsurance | 0.71 | [0.63, 0.79] |\n| 95% coinsurance | 0.67 | [0.59, 0.76] |\n\nThat is, about 25%, 29%, and 33% fewer visits than under free care. Averaged\nover the sample, expected visits fall from 3.3 a year under free care to 2.2\nunder 95% coinsurance. Even the upper ends of the worst-case intervals imply\nfewer visits than under free care in each of these plans, by at least 17% in\nthe 25% plan and at least 23% in the 95% plan.\n\nTwo cautions keep this reading within what was tested. First, the model\nenters coinsurance in logs, so the shape of the pattern across plans, with\nmost of the reduction at the first step away from free care, comes from that\nchoice of form; it was not estimated plan by plan. Second, the results say\nnothing about coinsurance rates the experiment did not offer, such as rates\nbetween 50% and 95% or above 95%.\n\nPlans with an individual deductible had about a quarter fewer visits than\nplans without one, holding the coinsurance rate fixed in the model (rate\nratio 0.74; on average,\n3.1 visits a year without a deductible and 2.3 with one).\n\nBecause plans were assigned at random, these differences can be read as\neffects of cost sharing rather than of who chose which plan. The controls\nbehave as expected: people with physical limitations or more chronic\ndiseases see the doctor more often.\n\n## Robustness\n\nThe **negative binomial** model allows visits to vary more than the Poisson\nmodel does, which matters here because visits vary about six times as much\nas a Poisson model assumes. It gives almost the same cost-sharing estimates\n(0.917 for coinsurance, 0.749 for the deductible), so the main result does\nnot depend on the Poisson assumption.\n\nThe model **without influential observations** gives larger effects (0.885\nand 0.618). This check should be read with care: it drops 3,346 person-years\n(17%), mostly heavy users averaging 8.1 visits a year, along with 737\nperson-years with no visits. It describes the effect among lighter users more\nthan it tests the stability of the main estimate. In all three columns the\ncost-sharing effects point the same way and are significant under the\nworst-case standard errors.\n\n## Exploratory analysis: people in poor health\n\n*Exploratory. This analysis was not in the pre-analysis plan and was chosen\nafter the confirmatory results had been seen. It can suggest a pattern but\ncannot confirm one.*\n\nPeople in poor health may have less room to cut back on doctor visits, so\ncost sharing might reduce their visits less. We added an interaction between\nlog coinsurance and poor self-rated health to the main Poisson model, keeping\nevery other choice the same.\n\n**Table 2 (exploratory). Coinsurance effect by poor self-rated health**\n\n| Estimate | IRR | 95% CI (robust) | p | 95% CI (worst case) | p (worst case) |\n|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|\n| Log coinsurance, not in poor health | 0.913 | [0.902, 0.925] | < .001 | [0.888, 0.940] | < .001 |\n| Log coinsurance, in poor health | 1.014 | [0.945, 1.088] | .70 | [0.866, 1.188] | .86 |\n| Ratio of the two | 1.110 | [1.034, 1.192] | .004 | [0.947, 1.301] | .20 |\n\nAmong people not in poor health, each unit of log coinsurance goes with about\n9% fewer visits. Among people in poor health, the estimate is close to no\neffect. The difference between the two is significant with robust standard\nerrors but not under the worst-case bound for repeated people. In predicted\nvisits, moving from free care to 95% coinsurance goes with 1.1 fewer visits a\nyear for people not in poor health, and 0.4 more for people in poor health.\n\nThis evidence is weak. Only 302 of 20,190 person-years (1.5%) are in poor\nhealth, and 207 of those are in the free-care plan, so the poor-health\nestimate rests on fewer than 100 person-years with any coinsurance. Poor\nhealth was not randomized, so people who rate their health as poor may differ\nin other ways that also shape how they respond to cost sharing. The pattern\nis consistent with the idea that sicker people cut back less, but it would\nneed confirmation in data with more people in poor health and with person\nidentifiers.\n\n## Limitations\n\nWithout a person identifier, the standard errors treat each person-year as independent, so they are too small. The plans ran for three or five years, and people may have delayed or moved care around the plan's start and end.\n\n## Conclusion\n\nCost sharing reduced outpatient doctor visits in the RAND Health Insurance\nExperiment. People facing 95% coinsurance made about a third fewer visits\nthan people with free care, and an individual deductible reduced visits by\nabout a quarter. The result holds under a worst-case allowance for repeated\nobservations of the same people and across both robustness checks. Data with\nperson and family identifiers would allow properly clustered standard errors,\nand a larger sample of people in poor health would be needed to test whether\ncost sharing affects them differently.\n\n## References\n\nAron-Dine, A., Einav, L., & Finkelstein, A. ## Code (`analysis/analysis.py`, `analysis/exploratory.py`)

**Main model and standard errors**
8. **First run with model-based Poisson standard errors**, the statsmodels default.
9. **Converting standard errors to the rate-ratio scale** by the delta method, rather than reporting them on the coefficient scale.
10. **Adding 95% confidence intervals and p-values** to Table 1 (not asked), and a constant row labeled "baseline rate."
11. **Checking for overdispersion** (Pearson χ²/df) without being asked. That check drove the switch to robust standard errors.
12. **Choosing HC0** among the robust standard-error types.
13. **The √5 bound:**
    - You approved my approach. I had proposed √(average years per person), about 1.8–2×.
    - After your approval I switched to √5, based on a maximum 5-year enrollment. That figure comes from outside sources, not from the data, and the change was mine.
14. **Showing the bound beside the robust standard errors**, rather than making it the primary standard error.
15. **Applying the bound to every column**, including both robustness checks.

16. **Restructuring Table 1** into stacked IRR / (robust SE) / [bound SE] cells, and dropping the CI and p-value columns.
17. **Basing the significance stars only on the bound standard error.**
18. **Column labels**, and reporting the negative binomial dispersion (alpha).

**Robustness checks**
19. **Running the Cook's distance check despite CLAUDE.md.** You said "keep both checks," and I took that as approval to override the keep-every-person-year rule for that check.
20. **How I computed Cook's distance:** from a GLM Poisson fit of the main model (the skill doesn't say which fit), then a refit as Poisson with robust standard errors.
21. **The negative binomial form (NB2)**, with HC0 standard errors and a 200-iteration limit.
22. **Stopping the script with an error if any model fails to converge.**

**Exploratory analysis**
23. **Poor health means `hlthp`** (poor self-rated health) only, not fair health.
24. **Interacting poor health only with `lncoins`**, not with `idp`.
25. **The combined poor-health effect** and its standard error, computed by the delta method.
